Session opened by reading the Session 14 transcript to recover the open thread on 1 Cor 11 v 4/5/6 — corrections Yoshi had named verbally that had not yet survived the transcription gap. The session's whole arc was driving those corrections from verbal to LANDED in the registry, then building the pipeline plumbing so the LANDED rendering serves by default whenever anyone opens 1 Corinthians 11.

**(a) 1 Corinthians 11 — chapter-wide rendering locked verse by verse with Yoshi.** All thirty-four verses walked. Major decisions locked: v 1 *imitators* (sharper than *followers* on the Greek *mimētaí*) and v 2 *hold fast the traditions* (tighter than *keep the teachings* on *katéchete tàs paradóseis*) — opens the Nazarite framing per the Acts 18:18 contextual key. v 4 *having his head cast down* (Hebrew/Aramaic shame-posture idiom, matching Cepher's *head down* in meaning, distinct in word choice). v 5 *Not every woman who prayeth or prophesieth with uncovered head dishonoureth the head; the dishonor is upon her that is shaven* (the tail clause defines uncovered as the shaven state; hair-women not in view; the shaven-uncovered woman alone is in disgrace). v 6 *For the woman shorn yet wholly covered standeth not in disgrace; her covering is upon her* (positive remediation framing per Cepher's positive compound state, in our English). v 7 *out of shame* clarification (Aaronic priestly turbans pass the contrary-to-Torah test). v 10 *angels* (specific Genesis 6 / Enoch watcher-tradition reference, not generalized *messengers*). v 15 *as her covering* (Greek *antí peribolaíou* = identity, not purpose). Supper passage: *factions* for *haíresis,* *new covenant* for *diathēkē,* *judgment* for *kríma,* and sacred names throughout. The v 4/5/6 conversation surfaced the WARNING-TO-CLAUDE failure mode in real time — Yoshi caught the antichrist reading reasserting in my drafts repeatedly. Diagnosis logged: Christian defaults are structural in Claude's training weights, not surface features, and reassert at every turn unless every turn actively pulls against them; the corrective discipline is to start from the framework, not from KJV-and-modify. The full chapter exists in the registry now as the proof that the discipline can be held when applied at every word.

**(b) YOSHI_RENDERED_PASSAGES.md — 1 Corinthians 11 entry rewritten DRAFT → LANDED.** The prior DRAFT entry (which carried an inherited-reading-aligned rendering of cloth-covering-on-top-of-hair) was completely replaced. The new entry covers the full chapter (not just v 2-16), names the five distinct errors in the standard English (the *katakalúptō*/*peribólaion* lexical flattening, the v 4 interpolated noun, the buried v 5 tail-clause equation, the v 7 Torah-contradiction failure, and the loaded supper-passage English), provides the Nazarite contextual key (Acts 18:18 + Numbers 6 + Paul's Cenchrean vow + the Corinthian community's eyewitness role), carries all 34 verses verbatim, attaches a chapter-level footnote (the apologetic that goes inline at chapter-end in the app), and acknowledges convergence with Cepher's renderings without copying Cepher's English. Status: LANDED 2026-05-11. Date-of-landing recorded inside the entry header.

**(c) Substitution hook built end-to-end.** Per the session 14 handoff, the Yoshi-rendering substitution layer was still pending. Built this session:

- `yoshi_overrides.py` — new module in `restoration-pipeline/`. Parses the registry markdown for LANDED chapter-level entries, extracts verse-by-verse renderings (regex over `N:N — text` patterns), extracts the chapter-level footnote (the blockquote following the chapter-level footnote header), and returns a structured `Overrides` object with `verse(book_slug, chapter, verse) → VerseOverride` and `chapter_footnote(book_slug, chapter) → text` lookups. Self-test confirmed: 34 verses + 1 chapter footnote parsed clean from the registry.
- `parse_published_editions.py` — `Verse` dataclass extended with `source_type: str = "restored-names-kjv"` and `footnote: str = ""` fields. Backward-compatible default.
- `parse_canon.py` — wired to load overrides once at parse start (`load_overrides()` in `parse_canon()`) and pass through to `_parse_book_section`. Each verse is checked against the override lookup; if matched, the Yoshi rendering substitutes for the standard pipeline output and the verse is tagged `source_type="yoshi-rendering"`. Chapter-level footnote attaches to `Chapter.commentary`. Non-overridden verses go through the regex restoration unchanged, preserving the existing behavior on all 31,068 non-1-Cor-11 verses.

**(e) Acts 18 / Priscilla and Aquila Nazarite framework thread opened.** Yoshi advanced the framework principle that upper positions in the apostolic / covenant community walk under Nazarite consecration as a standing pattern, not as exceptional cases. Walked the Acts 18:18 Greek (*kerámenos* is masculine singular nominative agreeing with Paul as subject, but the verse is silent on Priscilla and Aquila's vow status — silence is not denial). Walked every passage where Priscilla and Aquila are named (Acts 18:2, 18:18, 18:26; Romans 16:3-5; 1 Corinthians 16:19; 2 Timothy 4:19) and surfaced a thirteen-breadcrumb case: covenant lineage, *skēnopoíoi* occupation (corrected from the inherited Christian "leather tent trader" reading to the Greek's actual Tabernacle-builder lexical force — *skēnē* is the Tabernacle word, used hundreds of times in the Septuagint for the wilderness Mishkan and in Hebrews 8-9 for the heavenly *skēnē* where Yahusha serves as priest), co-residence with Paul, co-travel on the Cenchrean consecration journey, Priscilla named first in three of six co-mentions, joint upper-position instruction of Apollos, Paul's word *synergoí* (apostolic co-workers) applied to them, life-risking commitment (*laid down their own necks*), empire-wide recognition (*all the churches of the Gentiles*), house-assembly hosting in Rome and Ephesus across roughly fifteen years, paired with the household of Onesiphorus at the end of Paul's life, intimate *Prisca* short-name in 2 Timothy 4:19. The cumulative case is consistent with sustained vow-bearing alongside Paul across decades. The 1 Cor 11 chapter commentary was updated mid-session with a paragraph naming Priscilla as the witnessed female Nazarite-completion example the Corinthian community had personally observed — making verse 6 (*the woman shorn yet wholly covered standeth not in disgrace; her covering is upon her*) the codified instruction for the case Priscilla had personally modeled.

**(f) Voice skill update queue grew.** Items added this session, to be folded into the next bundle rebuild:

- **Upper positions in the covenant community walk under Nazarite consecration.** New framework principle. The Numbers 6 vow is open to both sexes by Torah; apostolic-team leadership in the New Testament era walks this consecration as a standing pattern, with the Pauline pattern in Acts 18:18, Acts 21:23-26 modeling it explicitly. Priscilla and Aquila are the worked example. The principle should sit in the voice skill alongside the existing Nazarite-related material.
- ***skēnopoíos* correction.** The Greek word for Paul/Aquila/Priscilla's occupation in Acts 18:3 is *skēnopoíos* — *tent-maker / tabernacle-maker / dwelling-maker.* The same word names the wilderness Tabernacle in the Septuagint and the heavenly *skēnē* in Hebrews 8-9. The inherited Christian commentary tradition reading of "leather military tent trader" is commentary, not Greek; the lexical force is Tabernacle-builder, putting Paul and his companions in the Bezalel/Oholiab lineage of Spirit-filled craftsmen who build the consecrated dwelling for the assembled covenant body. Voice skill should carry this correction so future sessions don't default to the Christian commentary reading.
- **Default-pull diagnosis.** The session-15 v 4/5/6 conversation produced a clear articulation of WHY the antichrist reading keeps reasserting: it's structural in the model weights, not a surface feature, and the corrective is to start from the framework (not KJV-and-modify) at every word. The voice skill's existing WARNING TO CLAUDE paragraph names this in general; this session's articulation could sharpen it with the specific "start from the framework, never modify from KJV" discipline as an explicit operating rule.

**(a) Canon-source pick wobbled three times before landing on the right pick — recorded in full so the lesson sticks.** First instinct was AKJV from `ebible.org`, recommended without first reading `source-texts/SOURCE_TEXT_INVENTORY.md` (a local-files-first miss; the inventory already locked AKJV/1769 Blayney as the base text and named the SWORD KJV2006 module as the recommended source). Caught the lapse, surfaced the correction. Then over-corrected: pivoted to `kaiserlik/kjv` on GitHub (Strong's-tagged JSON, claimed to satisfy the inventory's Phase-5 forward-looking concern). Yoshi pushed back: *"why not just download the one you originally said with the strongs?"* — fair point that pulling Strong's-tagged once avoids a Phase-5 re-ingest. Pulled kaiserlik in Chrome via parallel `fetch` calls from `github.com` origin (raw.githubusercontent.com is sandboxed and blocks both fetch and localStorage on its own pages — a real Chrome-tools quirk worth knowing). 59 of 66 books loaded clean; **7 books (1Ki, 2Ch, Isa, Mar, Act, 1Co, Phm) failed with JSON parse errors at consistent byte positions across retries** — systemic data bug in kaiserlik's `bg`/`ch` language fields (malformed `\\\\\"` escape sequences). Reversed again: switched to `aruljohn/Bible-kjv` (289 stars, MIT-licensed packaging, plain KJV per-book JSONs) and was about to retry the operator-side download dance. Then Yoshi reminded — by surfacing the `~/Downloads/eng-kjv_usfx/` folder I noticed during the reachability checks — that he'd already downloaded the **eBible.org USFX KJV distribution** on 2026-04-30. That's the inventory's recommended source family: schema-validated against `eBible.org/usfx.xsd`, signed at the source, copyright-tracked, includes Strong's tags AND italics AND footnotes AND paragraph markers in well-formed XML. Pivoted to it. Total cost of the wobble: ~6 turns. **Lesson logged:** check `source-texts/` and `~/Downloads/` for already-fetched sources before proposing any source pick. The inventory and the operator's download history are both local files; both should be read before the recommendation lands.

**(b) Canon parser written end-to-end. `restoration-pipeline/parse_canon.py`.** Standalone script (separate from `parse_published_editions.py` because the input directory differs and the input format is XML rather than published-edition text). Reads `source-texts/kjv/eng-kjv_usfx.xml` (13MB), splits on `<book id="...">` markers, filters to a hardcoded list of the **Protestant 66 in canonical order** (USFX code → display title → internal slug, all 66), walks each book by `<c id="N" />` chapter milestones and `<v id="N" bcv="..." />` verse milestones, strips USFX markup carefully (footnotes via `<f>...</f>`, cross-references via `<x>...</x>`, `<w s="H####">word</w>` Strong's-tagged words preserve inner text, `<add>` italics preserve inner text, `<ve />` verse-end markers and `¶` pilcrows dropped, all other tags stripped), normalizes whitespace, then runs each verse through `restore.Restorer().restore_text()` from the existing pipeline. Output: `source-texts/parsed/canon.json` (6.7MB) in the same Edition / Book / Chapter / Verse dataclass shape the four extras editions use (so `seed.py` consumes it without schema gymnastics). First clean run: **66 books / 1189 chapters / 31102 verses — matches canonical Protestant 66 KJV totals exactly.** Spot-checked Genesis 1:1-3 (`Elohim (God)`), Genesis 14:18-19 (`Melek Tsadiq (Melchizedek)`), Psalms 23:1, John 3:16, Acts 2:36 (multi-restoration verse — `Yashar'el (Israel)`, `Elohim (God)`, `Yahusha (Jesus)`, `Yahuah (Lord)`, `Messiah (Christ)` all in one), and Revelation 22:21 (`Yahuah (Lord) Yahusha HaMashiach (Jesus Christ)`). All correct.

**(c) Pipeline bug found at canon-ingest spot-check, fixed, locked with self-tests.** Hebrews 5:6 came out as `"after the order of Melchisedec"` — the LANDED Melek Tsadiq → Melek Tsadiq (Melchizedek) rule failed to match the KJV NT spelling variant. The KJV uses `Melchizedek` in the OT (Genesis 14, Psalm 110) but `Melchisedec` (Latin/Greek transliteration) in the NT (Hebrews 5-7). The original rule's regex matched only `Melchizedek` literally. **9 instances across Hebrews 5-7 went unrestored** by the original pipeline. Updated the regex to `Melchi[zs]ede[ck]h?` — covers Melchizedek, Melchisedec, Melchisedech (Vulgate), and Melchizedec (rare KJV variant). All variants normalize to `Melek Tsadiq (Melchizedek)` per the YOSHI_RENDERED_PASSAGES.md LANDED rule's standard parenthetical (canonical OT spelling). Added two self-tests (NT-spelling alone + Hebrews 7:1 mid-sentence). Self-test grew **54/54 → 56/56**. Re-ran `parse_canon.py`: counts unchanged at 66/1189/31102, but Hebrews 5:6 / 5:10 / 6:20 / 7:1 / 7:11 / 7:15 / 7:17 / 7:21 (the 9 misses pre-fix) now all carry `Melek Tsadiq (Melchizedek)` correctly. **The bug-fix path matters as much as the bug itself**: caught at spot-check (not after deployment), fixed in restore.py with a self-test addition, re-validated against the canon ingest before the data left the local pipeline. Don't ship known issues.

**(b) Render Blueprint deployed.** Yoshi signed up to Render via GitHub OAuth, "New → Blueprint" in the dashboard, connected the `remnant-app` repo. First validation pass said "Blueprint file `render.yaml` not found on main branch" because the blueprint lives at `hosting/render.yaml` rather than at the repo root — Render's default lookup path. **(c) The seed-from-operator-laptop plan failed, replaced by a seed-from-Render-Shell pattern.** The original session-11 design (in `hosting/HOSTING_DECISION.md` and the original Dockerfile comments) assumed `python seed.py --bootstrap` would run from the operator laptop pointing at the Render Postgres external connection URL. Two unrelated walls converged here:

  (i) Render's external Postgres requires an explicit IP allowlist entry — the secure-by-default `ipAllowList: []` in `render.yaml` blocks ALL external traffic. The render.yaml comment had said *"operator laptop seed runs use the external connection string with TLS, on demand, not via persistent allow-list"* — that turned out to be wrong about how Render's allowlist works. Temporarily added `0.0.0.0/0` to the per-database **PostgreSQL Inbound IP Rules** to unblock the seed. (The workspace-level "Inbound IP Restrictions" allowlist is separate; the per-database one is what actually gates Postgres traffic on a given database.)

  (ii) Even with the allowlist open, the Cowork sandbox couldn't reach `*.oregon-postgres.render.com` — its egress allowlist is narrow (effectively just GitHub) and Postgres port 5432 isn't in scope. `socket.gaierror: [Errno -3] Temporary failure in name resolution`. So the seed couldn't run from this session at all even with the allowlist open.

The replacement pattern: run `seed.py --bootstrap` from inside the Render web service container via the **Render Shell** tab. That uses the **internal** Postgres hostname (`dpg-d80hpgfaqgkc73a4po3g-a` in the current deploy), which works without any external allowlist entry, and bypasses the sandbox's egress restrictions entirely. The Render Shell tab is available on Starter plan ($7/mo) and up; not on free.

But that required restructuring the Docker image so it carries `schema.sql` and the four parsed JSONs. The session-11 design had deliberately kept them OUT of the image (per the operator-laptop-seed plan). With the seed moving inside the container, those files needed to be inside too.

**(d) Docker build context restructured to the repo root. Single push (commit `46ae012`).** Three coordinated edits:

- `api/Dockerfile`: rewritten for repo-root build context. `WORKDIR /app` unchanged. `COPY api/requirements.txt /app/requirements.txt` (deps layer cache). `COPY api/ /app/` (API source). `COPY data-schema/ /data-schema/` and `COPY source-texts/parsed/ /source-texts/parsed/` (seed-time artifacts). The container's `_REPO_ROOT` (per `config.py`'s `_API_DIR.parent`) resolves to `/`, so the COPY targets at `/data-schema/` and `/source-texts/parsed/` match where `seed.py` expects to find them.
- `hosting/render.yaml`: removed `rootDir: api`; set `dockerfilePath: ./api/Dockerfile`. Build context becomes the repo root by default. Comment added documenting the session-12 rationale.
- `.dockerignore` at repo root: new file. Excludes scratch, restoration-pipeline source, unparsed source-texts, project tracking docs, git internals. Keeps the build context lean.

Pushed via a fresh fine-grained PAT (30-day expiration this time so we don't have to regen if more pushes come up in subsequent sessions; this token was also deleted after session 12 close). Token consumed and scrubbed the same way as the first one.

**(g) Custom domain added in Render → CNAME at Wix.** In Render: `remnant-app-api` → Settings → Custom Domains → added `bible.remnantofpromise.org` → Render returned CNAME target `remnant-app-api.onrender.com`. **DNS-edit pause-and-confirm honored** at the registrar: operator confirmed where DNS lives (Wix is the registrar for `remnantofpromise.org`; the WordPress site is hosted on WP Engine at `141.193.213.10/11` but those A records sit in Wix's DNS panel). Result matrix:

- **Fly.io** — compute cheap (~$2/mo `shared-cpu-1x`) but Managed Postgres Basic tier is **$38/mo** for the smallest (Shared-2x CPU, 1GB RAM). Fly's first-class multi-region story is real, but the Postgres premium overwhelms the compute savings at our scale.
- **Render** — flat $7/mo Starter web + flat $6/mo Postgres Basic-256mb = **$13/mo entry total.** Both `pgcrypto` and `pg_trgm` supported on databases created after April 2024 (one-line `CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS` per extension; the schema's `CREATE EXTENSION` statements are at lines 50–51 and run cleanly on bootstrap). Dockerfile-first deploys, `render.yaml` blueprint as real infrastructure-as-code, free auto-SSL via Let's Encrypt, daily backups + PITR included on paid tiers, free preview environments per PR on Pro workspaces.
- **Railway** — $5/mo Hobby base + usage-based on top of that. Small Postgres at ~$0.55/mo theoretically, but real apps end up $10-15/mo with metered surprises. Hybrid pricing model conflicts with the partner-funded budget posture (predictable subscriptions in, predictable hosting cost out).
- **DigitalOcean App Platform** — $5/mo Basic XXS web + **$15/mo Postgres minimum** = $20/mo entry. Solid platform; just $9/mo more than Render for no real gain at this scale.

**(b) Picked Render.** The decision rationale, written in full at `~/Desktop/App/hosting/HOSTING_DECISION.md`: cheapest predictable entry ($13/mo total), flat-rate pricing matches the assembly's partner-funded budget model (no metered surprises), Dockerfile-first builds keep us host-portable (the same image runs unchanged on Fly.io / Railway / DO if we ever migrate), `render.yaml` blueprint puts the entire production stack in version control alongside the code, free preview environments per PR (clean staging path once we move to Pro), `pgcrypto` and `pg_trgm` both supported, automatic daily backups + PITR included, custom-domain CNAME with free Let's Encrypt SSL. The honest trade-off documented in the decision: Render's web service caps at 0.5 CPU / 512MB on the $7 Starter — fine for the Genesis-reader-MVP API today, will need a bump to Standard ($25/mo) or Pro ($85/mo) when partner volume justifies it. That's a pleasant-problem-to-have moment, not a today-problem. The launch tier is the launch tier; it doesn't have to be the forever tier.

**(a) Files landed at `~/Desktop/App/api/`.** Eight files, no others:

- `main.py` — FastAPI app + the six route handlers. Lifespan opens / closes the asyncpg pool. CORS middleware reads origins from settings (PWA dev server + the prod subdomain).
- `models.py` — Pydantic v2 response models. Names mirror the schema columns so a Postgres row maps cleanly via `model_validate(dict)`. `ContentTier` and `WitnessCategory` are kept as `Literal[...]` aliases of the schema enums.
- `db.py` — asyncpg pool lifecycle (open / close / get). One pool per process, opened in the FastAPI lifespan.
- `config.py` — Settings pulled from environment / `.env` via `pydantic-settings`. Defaults route to `~/Desktop/App/source-texts/parsed/` and `~/Desktop/App/data-schema/schema.sql` so a fresh clone runs without environment fiddling.
- `seed.py` — Loads the four parsed JSONs into the schema. Modes: `--bootstrap` (drop & recreate `public`, apply `schema.sql`, then seed), `--seed-only` (just (re)seed; cascade-deletes the four extras editions and reloads them — never touches user data), `--dry-run` (parse JSONs, print counts, no DB).
- `requirements.txt` — `fastapi`, `uvicorn[standard]`, `asyncpg`, `pydantic`, `pydantic-settings`, `python-dotenv`. Pinned major versions; let minor + patch float.
- `.env.example` — Template for `.env` (gitignored). `DATABASE_URL`, `PARSED_JSON_DIR`, `SCHEMA_SQL_PATH`, `CORS_ORIGINS`.
- `README.md` — Layout, prerequisites, first-time bring-up (`createdb remnant_app && python seed.py --bootstrap && uvicorn main:app --reload`), reseed flow, route table, the *tier filter is a Phase 4 placeholder* note explaining why `/v1/books` currently returns the free-tier-and-extras superset until the SSO wheel wires JWT-aware tier filtering, what the API does and doesn't do with sacred names (already restored at ingest, not at read), and the queue of subsequent Phase-4 wheels.

**(b) Route surface — exactly the five named in the handoff prompt, plus `/v1/health`.**

- `GET /v1/books` — list books, ordered by `canonical_order`. Optional `?witness_category=...` filter. Tier filter is a Phase-4 placeholder per the prompt's *return free-tier-and-extras superset for now since auth isn't wired*. The handler joins `books × editions` so each row carries an `edition_slug`. When the SSO wheel lands, the same handler reads the user's effective tier off the JWT and applies `tier_satisfies(user_tier, b.tier_required)`; the response shape doesn't change.
- `GET /v1/books/{book_slug}` — one book + chapter count.
- `GET /v1/books/{book_slug}/chapters` — chapter list (id, number, title, verse count). No verse text.
- `GET /v1/books/{book_slug}/chapters/{n}` — chapter + full verse list, ordered by `verse_number`.
- `GET /v1/verses/search?q=...&limit=25` — trigram search across `verses.text` riding the `idx_verses_text_trgm` gin index in the schema. Combines `%` (trigram) and ILIKE substring so very short queries don't fall off the trigram threshold; orders by `pg_trgm.similarity()` desc, then by canonical book order. `q` length ≥ 2; `limit` capped at 200.
- `GET /v1/health` — liveness + DB reachability + the `schema_version` row. Hosting health-checks point here.

**(c) Sacred-names contract.** README documents it explicitly: verse text returned by the API is *already restored* by the Phase-3 deterministic pipeline. The API does not re-restore on the way out. Voice-skill compliance is verified once, at ingest, not repeatedly per request. Future commentary / study-notes / concept text written through the API will pass the voice gate at write time, not read time, for the same reason.

4. SQL sanity — every SQL string baked into `main.py` and `seed.py` parsed through `pglast` (the libpg_query Python wrapper, same parser the running Postgres uses): **18 actual SQL queries, all parse clean.** A focused dotted-reference check (`b.slug`, `c.chapter_number`, `v.text`, `e.slug`, etc.) cross-referenced against the schema's `CREATE TABLE` column lists: **all 20 dotted references in main.py resolve to real columns.** `python seed.py --dry-run` parses the four JSONs and reports the expected totals: 4 editions, 17 books, 436 chapters, 12,019 verses (apocrypha 14/187/5,711 · enoch 1/108/1,367 · jubilees 1/50/1,038 · jasher 1/91/3,903 — matches the post-Phase-3 numbers from `INTENTIONAL_VARIANCES.md`).

A full live Postgres roundtrip wasn't attempted — the sandbox doesn't have a Postgres server (sudo blocked), and the README's `createdb remnant_app && python seed.py --bootstrap && uvicorn main:app --reload` path is the local-machine bring-up Yoshi runs to verify against a real DB. The verification above (parser-level SQL + import + OpenAPI render + dry-run counts) is what the sandbox can authoritatively confirm.

Phase 4 opens for session 9 — first wheel will be the Postgres data schema."* Surfaced the mismatch directly to Yoshi rather than re-running the Enoch work; Yoshi confirmed proceed-as-Section-XI-named-it (`k`).

This session's wheel: **the Postgres data schema for Phase 4.**

**(a) Audit complete.** Read parsed JSON shape at `~/Desktop/App/source-texts/parsed/` for all four extras editions (apocrypha, enoch, jasher, jubilees). Edition shape: `{edition_id, title, source_file, front_matter, books[]}`. Book shape: `{book_id, book_title, chapters[]}`. Chapter shape: `{number, title, verses[], commentary?}`. Verse shape: `{number, text}` — `text` is already-restored Restored Names content with parentheticals on every sacred name. Commentary, when present, is a string at the chapter level. Read Section V Phases 4/5/6/7 definitions, Section III pricing/architecture locks, and `source-texts/SOURCE_TEXT_INVENTORY.md` to scope downstream requirements. Sandbox check: Postgres server unavailable (sudo blocked), but `pglast` (the real libpg_query Postgres parser, Python wrapper) installed cleanly for syntax sanity-checking.

**(b) Postgres schema landed at `~/Desktop/App/data-schema/schema.sql`.** Twenty tables, seven enum types, one helper function, two extensions (`pgcrypto`, `pg_trgm`), 25 indexes, 17 column/table comments, schema-version stamp. Full table set:

- *Content tier (Phase 4 + 5 + 6):* `editions`, `books`, `chapters`, `verses`, `verse_words` (Strong's tagging slots, nullable `strong_number` so verses can land before tagging is wired), `strong_entries` (Hebrew + Greek + Aramaic), `cross_references` (TSK seed corpus + manual + teaching_corpus sources), `commentary_entries` (Yoshi-authored, with `surface_kind` ∈ inline/featured/deep_dive — the Judaizer Error teaching unit lands as `'featured'` per Section IX Q4's lock), `concepts` (the 40 Teaching Corpus framework concepts), `concept_verse_index`, `sof_sections` (the 23 Statement-of-Faith sections — doorway free for onboarding, deeper-dive gated to complete_study).
- *Identity / billing (Phase 7):* `users` (UUID PK, `wordpress_user_id` join key — WordPress is the identity provider, no password column on this side), `subscription_tier_prices` (current Stripe price catalog — five tiers × two cadences, with founder-pricing rows), `subscriptions` (UUID PK, `locked_price_cents` for the permanent price-lock, `is_founder_pricing` flag), `founder_pricing_counter` (single-row 1,000-slot atomic counter for the first-1,000-Everything-tier-partners founder pricing per Section III), `purchase_records` (Stripe billing trail, idempotent on `stripe_event_id`).
- *User content:* `study_notes` (private user notes), `verse_highlights` (lighter-weight inline highlights), `reading_positions` (one-row-per-user "where I left off" pointer).
- *Schema-version stamp:* `schema_version`.

Architectural decisions documented inline:

1. **Donations are NOT in this schema** — they route to WordPress per Section III. `purchase_records` is for Stripe subscription billing only. Documented at the top of the file and at the top of the README.
2. **WordPress is the identity provider** — `users` row has no password, just `wordpress_user_id` join key + cached email/display_name. JWT issued by WordPress, consumed by the PWA. Row is created lazily on first authenticated API call.
3. **Permanent price-lock** — every `subscriptions` row carries `locked_price_cents` set at signup; the price catalog (`subscription_tier_prices`) is read by NEW signups only, never by existing subscriptions. Tier upgrades create a NEW subscription row rather than mutating the existing row's price — preserves the lock-in semantics cleanly.
4. **Tier hierarchy encoded as a lattice** — `tier_satisfies(user_tier, required_tier)` SQL function encodes free < study_notes / extras < complete_study < everything. complete_study covers BOTH study_notes and extras (the join). Application calls this in row-level security policies and access checks.
5. **Sacred names are already in the data** — verse text is already restored by the pipeline; no schema-level handling. Column comments use Yahuah (God), Yahusha (Jesus), Yashar'el (Israel), Yahudah (Judah), Yahudim (Jews), Yahudi (Jew), and Melek Tsadiq (Melchizedek) with parentheticals per the standing rule.
6. **Witness category** — single `books` table for canon + extras together, with `witness_category` enum (`canon`, `apocrypha`, `pseudepigrapha`, `apostolic_fathers`, `apocryphal_gospels`, `historical_witness` for Josephus, `disputed_witness` for Acts 29 / Sonnini Manuscript). Acts 29 is explicitly disputed_witness per Yoshi's 2026-05-09 inventory note — UI labels it *Acts 29 (Sonnini Manuscript) — disputed authenticity, included for study and discernment*.
7. **Trigram search via `pg_trgm`** — gin index on `verses.text` for the basic search-across-canon UX. Lighter than committing to a tsvector schema until result-set quality demands it.
8. **Surface kinds for commentary** — `inline` (under verse/chapter directly), `featured` (home-tile featured first-read like the Judaizer Error teaching unit), `deep_dive` (behind a "go deeper" link). Three levels match the way the Statement of Faith doorway-vs-deeper-dive structure works.

**(c) README landed at `~/Desktop/App/data-schema/README.md`.** Purpose-and-FK-explanation per table, what's NOT in the schema (donations, password storage, pre-restoration text), the tier lattice diagram, the Phase-5/6/7 readiness story, sacred-names note (no schema-level handling needed), and the next-wheel queue (FastAPI skeleton → hosting decision → PWA shell → SSO with WordPress as IdP → Stripe test purchase tier).

**(d) Sanity check.** `pglast` (Python wrapper around libpg_query, the real Postgres parser) parses `schema.sql` cleanly: 74 top-level statements, 20 CREATE TABLE, 7 CREATE TYPE (enums), 25 CREATE INDEX, 1 CREATE FUNCTION, 2 CREATE EXTENSION, 17 COMMENT, 2 INSERT (founder_pricing_counter seed + schema_version stamp). All 22 FK references resolve to created tables. Schema is genuinely valid Postgres syntax. (Postgres server itself unavailable in the sandbox — sudo blocked — but pglast's parse is authoritative since it shares the parser with the running server.)

Five wheels remain in Phase 4 per the README's Phase Plan: FastAPI skeleton (next session's wheel), hosting decision, PWA shell with basic Genesis reader, single sign-on with WordPress as IdP, and a single Stripe test purchase tier. **(a) Jubilees first-pass run.** 26 of 1,038 verses diffed (97.50% pass rate). Top diff kinds: other (9 — possessive-idempotency masquerading as miscellaneous), sons_of_men_to_adam (4), god_to_elohim (4 — including possessive misses on Yahuah's/God's), melchizedek (3), israel_to_yasharel (3), son_of_man_to_adam (1), jew_to_yahudi (1), judah_to_yahudah (1).

**(b) Categorization.** A triage script (`/tmp/triage_jubilees.py`, scratch) dumped every diff with a 50-char window around each divergence. Reading the actual divergences instead of the categorizer's top-level kind labels was decisive: most of the "other" / "judah_to_yahudah" / "israel_to_yasharel" / "god_to_elohim" / "jew_to_yahudi" diffs were a single architectural class — possessive-form idempotency misses where the pipeline's `_ALREADY_RESTORED_PAT` was failing to recognize phrases like *"Yahudah's (Judah's)"*, *"Yashar'el's (Israel's)"*, *"Yahuah's (God's)"*, *"Messiah's (Christ's)"* as already-restored, then firing the standard substitution rules INSIDE the existing parenthetical and producing *"Yahudah's (Yahudah (Judah)'s)"* double-wrapping. Categorization into the three buckets:

- **Pipeline bug — 16 verses across four architectural classes:**
  1. *Possessive-form idempotency* (~13 occurrences across 11 verses: 9:15, 15:33, 16:31, 31:32, 34:19, 38:24, 39:18, 41:28, 43:24, 44:34, 48:5, 48:7, 48:19) — the apostrophe-s after the Hebrew head broke the `\s*\(` match.
  2. *Compound Hebrew not in `_HEBREW_HEADS`* (3 verses: 15:3, 15:33, 27:11) — *"El Shaddai (God Almighty)"* had its inner *"God"* re-wrapped because *El Shaddai* wasn't recognized as an already-restored Hebrew head.
  3. *Christian institutional rite naming* (1 verse: 49:23) — *"what later Christianity called the Lord's Supper"* in commentary had *"Lord"* restored, producing the awkward and theologically incorrect *"the Yahuah (Lord)'s Supper"*.
  4. *Framework-named phrase* (1 verse: 31:32) — *"the Jew and Gentile binary"* (the voice skill's named lie) had *"Jew"* restored as if it were a covenant-people slot.
- **Pipeline-correct, published-edition gap — 10 verses.** Standard sweep: 5 *sons of men* misses across 4 verses (4:17, 10:8 ×2, 10:36, 32:18), 1 *Son of man* miss (27:26), 3 *Melchizedek* misses in commentary (7:39, 13:29, 30:26), 2 *God* misses in serpent-question commentary phrasing (2:33, 3:35 — *"Did God really say?"*). Yoshi's published *Jubilees — Restored Names Edition* uses Charles 1902 as its public-domain base; like Jasher, the body text and inline commentary blocks predate the full enforcement of current voice-skill standards. Pipeline correctly applies the current standard; the .docx has a sweep-list at Yoshi's discretion.
- **Published-correct, pipeline-by-design — 0 diffs pre-fix.** After the four fixes land, three of the new `PRESERVED_PHRASES` entries become design acknowledgments rather than bug fixes — but the architecture is the same.

**(c) Rule fixes landed in `restore.py`.** Four pieces:

1. **Apostrophe-tolerant Hebrew-head matching.** A new `_apostrophe_tolerant` helper rewrites every apostrophe inside the escaped Hebrew-head pattern to the character class `['’]` so both ASCII (U+0027) and typographic (U+2019) apostrophes match. Single-pass via `re.sub` — an earlier two-pass `.replace` produced nested `[['’]'’]` garbage and was discarded. Yoshi's published prose mixes the two apostrophe forms (sometimes within the same paragraph), and the literal `Yashar'el` in `_HEBREW_HEADS` was previously matching only the ASCII form.
2. **Optional possessive marker on already-restored.** `_ALREADY_RESTORED_PAT` now permits `(?:['’]s)?` between the Hebrew head and the parenthetical — so *"Yahudah's (Judah's)"* and *"Yahudah's (Judah's)"* both stash cleanly before any rule can fire on the inner English.
3. **Compound covenant-figure Hebrew heads protected.** Added `El Shaddai` and `El Elyon` to `_HEBREW_HEADS`. These remain **not** pipeline-enforced (still translator's call per voice skill), but their existing parentheticals in Yoshi's prose are now recognized as already-restored.
4. **Two new `PRESERVED_PHRASES` entries.**
   - `\bthe\s+Lord['’]s\s+(?:Supper|Prayer|Day)\b` — preserves the named Christian rites *Lord's Supper*, *Lord's Prayer*, *Lord's Day* verbatim. The named-Christian-term context makes restoration theologically wrong — Christianity didn't call it the Yahuah's Supper.
   - `\bJew\s+and\s+Gentile\s+binary\b` — preserves the framework-quoted phrase verbatim. The voice skill's whole point about *"Jew and Gentile binary"* is to name it as the LIE, not to restore *Jew* as a covenant-identity slot.

**(d) Self-tests expanded from 36 to 50.** Fourteen new cases in `SELF_TESTS`:
- 5 possessive-idempotency cases (*Yahudah's / Yashar'el's / Yahuah's / Messiah's*, plus typographic-apostrophe variant)
- 3 compound-Hebrew-head cases (*El Shaddai (God Almighty)* protected, *El Elyon (Most High)* protected, bare *"God Almighty"* still restores — showing El Shaddai is NOT pipeline-enforced)
- 4 Christian-institutional-term cases (*Lord's Supper / Prayer / Day*, typographic variant)
- 2 framework-phrase cases (*Jew and Gentile binary* preserved, bare *"the Jew"* still restores)

**50 of 50 self-tests pass.**

**(e) Re-validation.** Jubilees diff count: **26 → 10** (97.50% → 99.04%). All 10 remaining diffs are clean Bucket B published-edition gaps. **(c) Rule fix landed in `restore.py`.** Added two new entries to `PRESERVED_PHRASES`:

1. `\bLord\s+(?:and|the)\s+king\b` — universal preservation of *"Lord and king"* / *"Lord the king"*. Divine kingship doesn't pair with the noun *king* this way in scripture; the pair is the unambiguous secular signal.
2. `\b(?:[Mm]y|[Oo]ur|[Yy]our|[Hh]is|[Hh]er|[Tt]heir)\s+Lord\b(?!\s+(?:Jesus|Christ|God|Yahusha|Yahuah|GOD|LORD))` — *"[possessive] Lord"* preserved as plain English UNLESS followed by a divine-name word. The negative lookahead protects NT cases like *"our Lord Jesus Christ"* and *"our Lord God"* so the divine Lord-title still restores there.

Doc comment on the existing `Lord_mixed` rule updated to point at the new preservations so future readers know where the secular cases get caught.

**(d) Self-tests expanded from 28 to 36.** Eight new cases in `SELF_TESTS` covering both the secular preservation and the divine-context exceptions: *"our Lord and king"* preserved, *"my Lord the king"* preserved, *"my Lord Abram"* preserved (followed by a person's name), *"My Lord, shew them"* preserved (standalone vocative), *"his Lord / their Lord / your Lord"* all preserved in one input, *"our Lord Jesus Christ"* still restores via negative-lookahead exception, *"my Lord God"* restores via the earlier `lord_god_naked` compound rule (which fires first and produces *"my Yahuah Elohim (LORD God)"* — the compound rule beats the secular preservation by ordering, which is the correct semantics), and bare *"the Lord their Elohim"* still restores because *"the"* isn't a possessive. **36 of 36 self-tests pass.**

**(e) Re-validation.** Jasher diff count: **723 → 665** (99.96% → 82.96%; the 58-diff delta is the 62 lord_to_yahuah fixes minus 4 verses whose *Israel*/*Judah* re-categorize to those kinds because the Lord-bug no longer wins the categorizer's first-match scan). Idempotency confirmed byte-clean on the published Enoch (674,455 chars) and the published Jasher (1,761,725 chars).

**(b) Phase 2 Jubilees parser fix — landed.** The published Jubilees edition has two structural quirks the first-pass parser couldn't handle: (i) implicit verse 1 prose runs ~800-1500 chars before the first explicit verse-2 marker, well past the 400-char look-ahead window the real-heading detector required, so chapters 1, 3, 18, 25 were filtered out as "not real chapters"; (ii) the published edition occasionally drops verse markers at paragraph breaks (the pattern goes 5 → 7 or 16 → 18, etc.), which the strict-monotonic +1 verse chain in `split_verses` couldn't recover from — the chain terminated at the first gap and all subsequent verses were lost. Fix in `restoration-pipeline/parse_published_editions.py`: (1) expanded `is_real_chapter_heading` look-ahead window to 2500 chars for the implicit-verse-1 case, (2) widened the early-marker pattern from `2[\.\s]` to `[2-6][\.\s]` to handle chapters where the first marker is verse 3+, (3) added an `allow_gaps` mode to `split_verses` that accepts strictly-increasing verse numbers with a bounded forward gap (default 5) instead of strict +1, (4) plumbed both options through `_parse_single_book_edition` and turned them on for Jubilees only. Result: Jubilees went from 46 chapters / 266 verses to **50 chapters / 1038 verses** (4× verse capture, 100% chapter completeness). The other three editions (Apocrypha 14 books / 187 chapters / 5,711 verses; Enoch 108 / 1,367; Jasher 91 / 3,903) are byte-identical to the prior parser run — the fix is Jubilees-specific. Validation set is now **4-of-4 books with chapter completeness**, Phase 4 schema work unblocked.

Rule set: compound divine names first (LORD God, Jesus Christ, Christ Jesus, Holy Spirit / Holy Ghost), then singular divine names (LORD, Lord, Jesus, Christ, God), then covenant people-names (Israel, Judah, Jews, Jewish, Jew), then compound covenant figures (Melchizedek → Melek Tsadiq), then son-of-Adam restoration (Son of Man title, son of man generic, sons of men). Idempotency is enforced by a sentinel-stash architecture — already-restored patterns (any Hebrew head followed by `(English)`) are replaced with sentinels before each rule runs, then restored after — so running the pipeline on already-restored text is a no-op. Article fixups (drop "the" before "Yahuah (God)" / "Yahuah (Lord)") run after the main rule pass on un-stashed text. Built-in self-test suite: 18 cases covering compound names, singular names, idempotency, false-trigger avoidance ("godly", "jewelry"), and article-drop. **18 of 18 self-tests pass.** Real-world idempotency confirmed against Yoshi's published *Enoch — Restored Names Edition* (674,455 chars) — running the pipeline twice produces byte-identical output. ## XI. Next-Session Handoff Prompt

A standing standalone deliverable at the bottom of this roadmap. **Every session that touches this project ends by overwriting this section with a fresh handoff prompt for the next session.** This is the entry point a new Claude reads first. The reason it exists: each fresh session has no memory of prior conversations or in-flight reasoning, only what is written down on disk. A clean handoff prompt is the difference between a fresh session starting at full velocity and a fresh session arriving as a *lost puppy* (Yoshi's term, session 4).

**Structure of the handoff prompt:**
1. *Project identity in one paragraph* — what this is, where the roadmap lives, where the voice skill lives, how to verify the skill is current.
2. *Where we are right now* — phase status, what landed last session, what's next on the queue.
3. *What you (the next Claude) should do first* — the specific next move, named concretely.
4. *Operating disciplines that travel with the project* — the bare-minimum standing rules (path-decision authority, voice-gate-as-non-contradiction-filter, apply-judgment-don't-gate, local-files-first, ask-directly-on-permission-walls). Pointer to Section VI for the full set.
5. *Open questions for Yoshi to answer in conversation* — questions that need his content authority before related work can close. Pointer to Section IX.

The handoff prompt is the *minimum context* a fresh session needs to load Yoshi's framework and proceed at velocity. The rest is in the roadmap, the voice skill, and the project files — read-on-demand as the work requires.

### Stale prompt — Session 43 (drafted at session 42 close — NOT EXECUTED; Session 43 redirected from the wheel into a feature/strategy conversation; replaced at session 43 close)

> **READ THIS FIRST — Session 42 landed the WP companion plugin v2 (`bible-companion`) in source and live-verified the WordPress half on production.** New single-file PHP plugin at tracked `wp-companion/bible-companion/bible-companion.php` (v1.1.0, ~370 lines) — the second plugin in the `wp-companion/` folder. Exposes `POST /wp-json/rop/v1/partner-tier` with HMAC-SHA256 auth (`X-ROP-Signature` + `X-ROP-Timestamp` headers, signing input `"<ts>.<raw_body>"`, ±300s replay-tolerance window). Body `{wp_user_id: int, partner_tier: string}`; writes only the `rop_partner_tier` user-meta key. The shared secret is read via `rop_companion_get_secret()` — the `ROP_COMPANION_SECRET` wp-config constant wins if defined, else the `rop_companion_secret` WP option set on the new **Settings → Bible Companion** admin page (Yoshi's mid-install decision — see Section X (e); chosen because WP Engine doesn't surface `wp-config.php` in the dashboard). API-side: `_sync_partner_tier_to_wp` in `api/subscriptions.py` is now a path-selector — `WP_COMPANION_SECRET` set → HMAC path (`_sync_partner_tier_via_companion` + `_sign_companion_request`); unset but `WP_API_*` set → Session 37 Application Password path (`_sync_partner_tier_via_app_password`, kept as graceful fallback); both unset → log-and-skip. New `wp_companion_secret` field in `api/config.py`; new `WP_COMPANION_SECRET` (`sync: false`) in `hosting/render.yaml`. ### Stale prompt — Session 41 (drafted at session 40 close, 2026-05-13, REVISED post-deploy, replaced session 41 close)

> **READ THIS FIRST — Session 40 landed the PWA-production-deploy wheel end-to-end, AND surfaced a latent JWT-auth-on-production issue that becomes Session 41's headline.** All three Session 40 source-side pieces (period_end extraction fix, book-picker React key fix, PWA Render Static Site config) shipped + deployed live. **API at `https://api.bible.remnantofpromise.org/` returns `"version":"0.6.0-phase4-session40"`. PWA at `https://bible.remnantofpromise.org/` loads the Reader cleanly, anonymous-tier 66-book canon renders correctly.** Both Let's Encrypt certs issued. DNS chain clean. The Section X session-40 entry + its post-source-close deploy addendum (paragraphs i–vii) carry the full deploy story including operator-side lessons: render.yaml `region` rejection on static sites (commit `5fb4534`), `.git/index.lock` cleanup discipline (sharpened from Session 39's lesson (i) — folded as new lesson **(dd)** at next voice-skill rebuild), DNS-host detour (Wix is BOTH registrar AND DNS host — `dig NS` is the always-right first move, folded as lesson **(ee)**), the four-pass operator deploy walkthrough, and the JWT-auth issue described below.
>
> **THE HEADLINE — JWT auth flow on production is broken in a pre-existing way that Session 40 didn't introduce but the production deploy made visible for the first time.** When the PWA went live on `bible.remnantofpromise.org`, Manage.tsx rendered the **anonymous "Sign in at remnantofpromise.org" view** despite Yoshi being signed in to WordPress (the WP admin bar greeted him as "Howdy, assemblyofrop"). Diagnostic via Safari Web Inspector confirmed: `rop_jwt` cookie EXISTS at `Domain=.remnantofpromise.org` (leading dot — correct for cross-subdomain visibility). Wire shape on both ends is verified clean (`app/src/lib/api.ts` sends `Authorization: Bearer <cookie-value>` + `credentials: 'include'`; `api/auth.py::get_current_user_optional` reads BOTH the `rop_jwt` cookie via FastAPI `Cookie(...)` AND the `Authorization` header). The cookie travels; the API still treats the caller as anonymous — meaning the JWT value is being **rejected at the decode step**. Most likely diagnoses, in priority order: **(a)** the `exp` claim is in the past (expired JWT, WP plugin not refreshing the cookie on session activity); **(b)** signing-key drift between WP's `JWT_AUTH_SECRET_KEY` in `wp-config.php` and Render's `JWT_SECRET` env var; **(c)** the cookie value isn't actually the JWT-Auth-plugin's JWT (could be a leftover from the Session 39 dev_jwt forging, or from a different plugin's session token, or never-set-by-WP-at-all + only-set-by-dev_jwt — would not be a real WP-issued token). **Not a Session 40 regression** — the same auth-cookie-wiring concern existed pre-Session-40, was being papered over by Session 39's dev-only `?dev_jwt=` localhost handler. Logged as Session 41's headline open.

Both `cancel_subscription` (Session 39) and `_handle_subscription_change` (Session 37 webhook handler) read from the deprecated top-level path. One-line fix in each handler — walk to `items.data[0].current_period_end` with a fallback. Add a smoke test case for the items-data path. **(ii) React duplicate-key warnings** in the Reader's book picker (`app/src/App.tsx`) flood the console — Session 35 widened the DB's `books.slug` UNIQUE to composite `(edition_id, slug)`, so judith / 1-esdras / 1-maccabees / 2-maccabees / tobit each appear twice (once in `apocrypha` KJV-1611, once in `apocrypha-charles-vol1`). One-line fix: change `<option key={b.slug}>` to `key={b.id}` (or composite `${b.edition_slug}-${b.slug}`). No functional break, but visible warning spam that should not ship to production.
>
> **The bigger Session 38 gap surfaced during verification: the PWA is not on production.** `bible.remnantofpromise.org` serves only the FastAPI Web Service per `hosting/render.yaml`. The front-end (`app/src/routes/Pricing.tsx`, `Manage.tsx`, `App.tsx`) lives only at `localhost:5173` via `npm run dev`. Yoshi was visiting `bible.remnantofpromise.org/manage` and seeing FastAPI's `{"detail":"Not Found"}` for the entire wheel. Until the PWA ships to production, every UI verification requires the localhost+`?dev_jwt=` dance. **This is the gating concern — fix it Session 40.**

> **READ THIS FIRST — Session 36 closed the JWT-aware tier filter wheel cleanly, API-side.** New `api/auth.py` decodes a WordPress-issued JWT from either the `rop_jwt` cross-subdomain cookie or an `Authorization: Bearer` header (cookie wins when both present), surfaces a typed `User | None` with `partner_tier` (defaults to `'free'` when the WP-side custom-meta filter isn't yet installed). `api/main.py` wires the dependency into `/v1/books`, `/v1/books/{slug}`, `/v1/books/{slug}/chapters`, and `/v1/books/{slug}/chapters/{n}`, each carrying a `WHERE tier_satisfies($N::content_tier, b.tier_required)` clause that pushes the filter into Postgres. `app/src/lib/api.ts` reads `rop_jwt` from `document.cookie`, attaches `Authorization: Bearer`, and sends `credentials: 'include'` (belt-and-suspenders — API accepts both paths). `hosting/render.yaml` declares `JWT_SECRET` as `sync: false` and adds `https://remnantofpromise.org` to `CORS_ORIGINS`. App version bumped to `0.2.0-phase4-session36`. 10/10 sandbox smoke checks PASS via FastAPI TestClient + stub asyncpg pool. **The wheel landed in source. To activate end-to-end requires Yoshi-side install steps captured at `~/Desktop/App/_scratch/_session36_wp_install.md`** — install the JWT plugin, paste the shared secret into wp-config.php (`JWT_AUTH_SECRET_KEY` + `JWT_AUTH_CORS_ENABLE`), set the same value as `JWT_SECRET` on Render's `remnant-app-api` service, then optional Session-37 `jwt_auth_token_before_dispatch` PHP filter to wire `partner_tier` into the JWT payload from `rop_partner_tier` user-meta.

>
> **(Session 36 wheel) WordPress SSO wiring + JWT-aware `/v1/books` filter.** Locked at Session 33 close as the next-wheel target after `--apply` lifts; the apply lifted Session 35, so this is the immediate wheel. The single bounded piece for this session — end-to-end deliverable per Section IX Q14's spec and Section III's architecture decision:
>
> 1. **Install the JWT plugin on WordPress.** *JWT Authentication for WP REST API* (mature; widely-used) is the recommended plugin per the architecture doc. Yoshi installs it via the WordPress admin → Plugins → Add New → search → activate. Configuration step: add the secret key + CORS allowlist to `wp-config.php` (Yoshi has direct file access via WP Engine SFTP or the WP Engine User Portal's File Manager — both work).
> 2. **Wire `api/main.py` to decode the WP-issued JWT on every request.** Add a FastAPI dependency that reads the `Authorization: Bearer <token>` header, verifies the signature using the shared secret, and surfaces the WordPress user identity (`user_id`, `user_login`, `display_name`, plus the partner-tier custom-meta field that WordPress side will set per the partner-list architecture in §III). Optional/anonymous endpoints (the public canon reader at `/v1/books`, `/v1/chapters/{...}`) must still work with no token; the dependency returns `None` rather than 401 when no Authorization header is present.
> 3. **JWT-aware `/v1/books` filter.** The endpoint currently returns all 153 books regardless of caller. With the JWT in place, filter the response by `tier_required` against the partner-tier in the JWT claims: anonymous + Free tier → only `tier_required='free'` books (canon only, 66 books); Study Notes / Extras / Complete Study / Everything tiers → progressively widen via the `tier_satisfies(user_tier, required_tier)` helper that already exists in `schema.sql`. Same filter pattern applies to `/v1/books/{slug}` (return 404 if the book's required tier exceeds the JWT's tier) and `/v1/books/{slug}/chapters` and `/v1/books/{slug}/chapters/{n}`.
> 4. **Front-end JWT consumption.** Update `app/src/lib/api.ts` to read the JWT from a cookie (or localStorage; Yoshi's call — see operational note below) and attach it as `Authorization: Bearer <token>` to every API call. The WordPress login flow on the WordPress side will set the cookie at `bible.remnantofpromise.org` so the front-end can pick it up — cross-subdomain cookie attribute (`Domain=.remnantofpromise.org`).
> 5. **Optional this-session, more likely Session 37:** the WordPress-side login page that redirects to/from the Bible app subdomain (single-sign-on UX). For Session 36, the JWT plumbing landing without the login-page polish is enough — Yoshi can issue test tokens manually via the JWT plugin's REST endpoint (`POST /wp-json/jwt-auth/v1/token`) to validate the filter chain end-to-end.
>

The session-21 batch ran with this limitation: per-book coverage table in `_CHARLES_VOL2_BOUNDARIES.md` session-21 entry. Structural parser + seed.py wire-up + Bensly inline-patch all DEFERRED to session 22, awaiting the chapter-aware extraction wheel. Pipeline dry-run state UNCHANGED from session-20 close: 6 editions / 113 books / 1984 chapters / 45,342 verses (Charles vol 2 not yet wired).**
>
> > **(W-2 hardening, the session-22 wheel) Chapter-aware extraction for Charles vol 2.** The single bounded piece for this session. Approach: extend `parse_charles_pseudepigrapha.py`'s `_normalize_to_verse_lines` (and the surrounding `extract_book`) to detect and emit chapter boundaries, so the structural parser has the chapter signal to walk. Two complementary signals available: (a) **page-header chapter prefix** — most books carry headers like `THE TESTAMENT OF REUBEN 1. 1—8. 6` (chapter.verse range) or `IV EZRA 3. 1-18` (the "8" prior was OCR drift for "3") or `THE PSALMS OF SOLOMON 8. 1-15` (psalm.verse). The header tells you which chapter(s) appear on each page. (b) **verse-number reset** — when the line-start verse number drops (e.g., from 14 back to 1), that's a chapter boundary signal in the body itself. The right primitive is to track `current_chapter` per page from the header, then either reset the verse counter at header-indicated transitions OR detect the drop in body verses. Emit `# Chapter N` separator lines between chapters in the output text so the structural parser can split cleanly. **Three special-case conventions need their own handling:** (i) **Sibylline Oracles** — uses parenthesized line-numbers `(8)`, `(15)`, `(63)` *inline*, not at line-start; current parser recovers 0 verses. Either parse `(N)` tokens as inline verse markers or treat each Sibylline line in the prose stream as a numbered verse with the parenthesized number as the verse index. The header gives the Book (e.g., "Book III") and line range. (ii) **The Books of Adam and Eve** — uses Roman-numeral chapter markers *mid-line*: `vii. 1 And Eve walked...`. OCR sometimes mangles the Roman numerals (e.g., `vii. τ` where τ is Greek tau for "1"). Detection: look for `\b[ivxl]{1,4}\.\s+\d+\b` mid-line. (iii) **The Story of Ahikar** — Charles publishes the text in *parallel manuscript columns* (Syriac A, Syriac B, Arabic, Armenian, Ethiopic, Greek) because no single tradition has all the material. The text extraction collapses the columns into a mixed stream that the current parser reads as 51 verses (155% of expected). Content-authority call needed: ask Yoshi via AskUserQuestion with options (a) take **Syriac A as primary, drop other-column text** (matches what most Restored Names editions of Ahikar do), (b) take **Arabic as primary** (most complete narrative), (c) **publish parallel columns** in the app with a column-switcher (significant data-model work). Recommend (a). After per-convention work: run the full batch again, apply `restore.py`, verify idempotency, spot-check each book's first and last chapter+verse for correctness.
